Larry's ten timings, each named as "Larry's timing N" in the PR that measures it:

| # | Larry's timing | PR |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | From the request to the LLM to its response | R96 |
| 2 | From the response arriving to the video and voice's first words | R96 |
| 3 | From one sentence spoken to the next one starting | R96 |
| 4 | Processing a PDF to make the wiki | R96a |
| 5 | Answering a question from the wiki | R96a |
| 6 | Preparing the video and voice | R96b |
| 7 | The first load of everything (least important, since it happens once, but sped up where there is a chance) | R96c |
| 8 | Processing a photo when it is added | R96b |
| 9 | Processing each recording when it is added | R96b |
| 10 | From the app starting to the LLM being ready | R96c |

Bullets marked **Added (from the code)** are further waits found in the code that a user notices; Larry didn't list them.

### R96. Where the time goes: a reply (Medium) · Speed · Done in [PR #138](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/138)
- An experiment that measures, on Larry's hosted machine (4 Ampere cores, llama-server with Gemma 3 4B on 2 threads) and in a cloud session, where the time goes in a reply, from the prompt sent to the last word heard. The existing timing readout gives these numbers without the browser; the results are kept in `experiments/speed/`, as for R96a to R96c.
- **Larry's timing 1:** from the request to the LLM to its response: the LLM's first token, first sentence and last token, with llama-server's own timings (prompt tokens read and their speed, prompt tokens reused from its cache, tokens written and their speed). Measured with the local LLM only: Larry decided on 2026-10-22 that the speed tests use llama.cpp alone for now, and other LLMs are revisited later.
- **Larry's timing 2:** from the first sentence arriving to the first word heard: the voice's first sound, the video's first frame, the clip ready, and the clip playing in the browser.
- **Larry's timing 3:** from each sentence's clip ending to the next one starting (the gap heard between sentences), for every sentence of the reply, with the next clip's voice and video times beside it, so a gap shows whether the next clip was late or the browser was.
- **Added (from the code):** the wait before the prompt reaches the LLM: a prompt sent right after a sample is accepted waits for the replies' warm-up (`wait_for_warm_up`, logged as `Prompt waited for the warm-up`), a prompt waits behind the one before (`chat_lock`) or the wiki's current LLM request (`llm_busy`, which R99 removes), and the voice and video engines can be held by a sample or greeting being rendered again (`engines()`).
- **Added (from the code):** the prompt's length: the tokens the history and the wiki's text add as a chat goes on, and how many llama-server reuses, since a longer prompt delays the first token.
- **Added (from the code):** the browser's own waits: it asks for new clips every 300 ms (`CLIP_POLL_MS`), so a ready clip can wait up to that long before it is fetched; then the clip's download, and the switch from the idle video to speaking and back at the end.
- **Added (from the code):** the push-to-talk microphone (R24): from releasing the button to the prompt sent, as the browser's speech recognition finishes.
- **Added (from the code):** Stop: from pressing Stop to silence, and to the next prompt being taken.
- **Can show:** a table of where the time goes in three replies, one of them spoken with push-to-talk, with Larry's timings 1 to 3 and the added ones, and the largest costs named; these decide R99 and R100.
- **Built:** `imageskin time-reply` sends prompts to the running app over its chat API, as the browser does but without one, follows each reply's clips, downloads each the moment it is listed, and plays the reply back on paper: clip 1 plays once its first bytes arrive, and each next clip once it has arrived and the one before has ended. It writes the tables as Markdown and JSON to `speed` in the data folder (`/data/speed` on the hosted machine) and prints them. `--spoken N` sends prompt N as if said with Hold to talk, so the server's correction of a spoken prompt is timed (the browser's own speech recognition can't be timed without the browser); `--stop` sends the last prompt again and presses Stop at its first clip, timing Stop's answer and when the stopped prompt's request finished. For this the chat API now returns each reply's `timings` (the warm-up and turn waits, the history's tokens, the first token, first sentence and last token, and llama-server's own `timings`, which it sends with the last piece of a streamed reply), and each clip in the clip list says when its voice step began and when its video was whole; the log has `Reply timings`. The browser no longer asks for clips every 300 ms: since R22a it waits on the server, which answers the moment a clip is listed, so the "Seen" column, against "Listed", is that wait. The results go in `experiments/speed/`.

### R96d. A spoken question checked sooner (Simple) · items 9, 12 · Done in [PR #140](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/140)
- Added by Larry on 2026-10-22, right after R96, whose run on his hosted machine found a spoken question reached the LLM 32.4 s after it was sent: before it is answered, the LLM was asked to put back misheard names with all 100 of the content's words, about 400 tokens read at 14 tokens a second.
- Only the content's words spelled like a word heard, or like two heard words run together, are named, at most 8 (compared with and without their first letters, as a first sound is often misheard: "Boiler" for "Euler"), and the LLM answers with just the changes ("Oiler -> Euler", or NONE), which are made here and kept only as `kept` already allows. The instruction's fixed start comes first, so llama-server can reuse it once R97 lets it.
- **Can show:** the check before and after against Gemma 3 4B on 2 threads, with its time and result for a few questions, and the log's `Spoken question corrected` or `Spoken question kept` with `suspects` and `duration_ms`.

### R24e. Voices that fit the faces (Medium) · items 58, 60 · Done in [PR #142](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/142)
- Added by Larry on 2026-10-22: the default people's voices don't match their pictures. A check of the 68 found why: the voice is picked only by gender and accent (`VOICES` in `default_people.py`), so all 30 women speak in `af_heart` or `bf_emma`, all 30 men in `am_michael` or `bm_george`, and the 8 nonbinary people in the blend of the two, whatever the face's age. The faces run from sixteen to the seventies, so these don't fit:
  - **10 teenagers, aged sixteen, with an adult's voice:** `photoreal_female_teenager`, `photoreal_female_teenager_2`, `photoreal_male_teenager`, `photoreal_male_teenager_2`, `animated_female_teenager`, `animated_female_teenager_2`, `animated_male_teenager`, `animated_male_teenager_2`, `photoreal_nonbinary_teenager` and `animated_nonbinary_teenager`.
  - **14 people in their sixties or seventies with the same voice as the twenty-year-olds:** `photoreal_female_professor_2`, `photoreal_female_parent_2`, `photoreal_female_boss_2`, `photoreal_male_office_manager_2`, `photoreal_male_parent_2`, `photoreal_male_business_consultant_2`, `animated_female_business_consultant_2`, `animated_female_professor_2`, `animated_female_office_manager_2`, `animated_male_parent_2`, `animated_male_boss_2`, `animated_male_high_school_teacher_2`, `photoreal_nonbinary_professor` and `animated_nonbinary_boss` (the people `looks_shown.json` marks older).
  - **Everyone else:** the 15 women of a look, from a Student to a Professor in her late fifties, all sound like the same woman, and the same for the men.
- Each person gets a voice of their own for each accent, kept with them (`Person` gets `voices`, or a table keyed by the person's name), instead of one for each gender and accent: a younger voice for the teenagers, an older one for the people in their sixties and seventies, and the adults spread over several of Kokoro's voices of their gender and accent (American `af_*` and `am_*`, British `bf_*` and `bm_*`), so faces next to each other on the avatar page don't sound alike. Kokoro has no teenage or older voices of its own, so each is the closest voice, a blend of two, or a speed (Kokoro's `speed`), picked by listening and by measured pitch, as R24b3 picked the nonbinary blend; the choices and the reasons are recorded in `tools/default_people/README.md`. A nonbinary person's voice stays a blend, of voices that fit the face's age.
- The packs don't change, since the voice isn't in them (Start now renders Goodbye and Welcome back in the voice when the person is chosen), so nothing is uploaded to the hosted machine again. A person already chosen gets the new voice the next time the app starts, and Goodbye and Welcome back are rendered again in it. **No recordings?** in setup (R24c3) offers the same voices; a ready-made voice saved before the change still works.
- **Can show:** a page with every one of the 68 faces beside a short line spoken in its American and British voice, for Larry to listen to; the teenagers sounding young, the older people older, and no two neighbouring faces on the avatar page sounding the same; a unit test that every person has a voice for each accent and that each voice Kokoro is given exists in its list; and the log's `Default voice chosen` with the person and the voice.
- **Built:** `VOICE_CHOICES` gives each gender, accent and age (younger, adult or older) its voices, picked by measured median pitch, as no voice was picked by listening in this session; within each look, in the avatar page's order, the people of a gender and age take them in turn, so no face sounds like either of the two before it. No speed change was needed, so a voice is still just a name or a blend. The lines' folder is named for the voice too, so at start-up a chosen person whose voice changed has Goodbye and Welcome back rendered again in it (`Default person's lines rendered again in their voice`). **No recordings?** lists every person's voice, from the server, such as "British woman, older 1". `tools/default_people/hear_voices.py` makes the listening page; the pitches and reasons are in `tools/default_people/README.md`.

### R96a. Where the time goes: the wiki and answers from it (Medium) · Speed · Done in [PR #145](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/145)
- The same experiment for the content, on the same machines: a sample PDF of about 20 pages and five questions about it. `imageskin time-pdf <file>` gives the PDF's numbers without the browser; the sample PDF and the results are kept in `experiments/speed/`.
- **Larry's timing 4:** processing the PDF to make the wiki: uploading it, reading it (text, slides, tables and math), then each wiki part and outline step, with llama-server's own timings for each request, and the total.
- **Larry's timing 5:** answering a question from the wiki: looking it up in the wiki and the PDFs (R39), the prompt with the wiki's text, then the LLM's first sentence and the first word heard (timings 1 and 2 for an answer from the content).
- **Added (from the code):** a question asked while the wiki is being built, which waits for the wiki's current request; and the next reply after the wiki finishes, which may have to read the system prompt again before llama-server reuses it (`wiki.when_idle`).
- **Added (from the code):** the longer requests built on the wiki: "What can you talk about?", Teach me, Quiz me and the assessment, each to its first word heard.
- **Added (from the code):** the Manage Content and wiki pages ask for progress every 1 and 2 seconds (`READING_POLL_MS`, `WIKI_POLL_MS`), so the progress shown can lag by that much; and replacing a PDF, which rebuilds only its pages.
- **Can show:** a table of where the time goes for the sample PDF and the five questions, with Larry's timings 4 and 5 and the added ones, and the largest costs named; these decide R97 and R98.
- **Built:** `imageskin time-pdf <file> [questions]` adds the PDF to the running app over its content API, as the Manage Content page does, waits for it to be read and its wiki built, asks the first question while the wiki is being built, then times each question and "What can you talk about?", "Teach me about", "Quiz me on" the first topic and "Assess me" as `time-reply` times a reply, and removes the PDF again (`--keep` keeps it). `--spoken N` sends question N as if said with Hold to talk, and `--replace` then replaces the PDF with itself and times its rebuild. The wiki now saves each part's and outline step's time with its pages, with llama-server's own timings for each of its requests (tokens read, reused from the cache and written, and their speed), and `Wiki pages built` in the log has `llm_requests`, `llm_prompt_n`, `llm_cache_n` and `llm_predicted_n`; a reply's `timings` also have `looked_up_ms` (the lookup in the wiki and PDFs) and `asked_tokens` (the prompt with what was found). The sample PDF (19 pages, made by `make_sample_pdf.py`), its five questions and the results are in `experiments/speed/`. Not timed: the progress pages' polling (`READING_POLL_MS`, `WIKI_POLL_MS`), which only delays what the browser shows by at most 1 and 2 seconds. On Larry's hosted machine the sample PDF's wiki took 3 hours against about 1 in the cloud, because the app there counted tokens by bytes (llama-server answers `/tokenize` with 503 while it loads the model, and the app then stops asking it), which made the wiki's parts a third of the size. [PR #148](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/148) fixed that: a 503 is now taken as a server that isn't up yet, and the count is asked of it again next time.

### R96b. Where the time goes: setup (Medium) · Speed · Done in [PR #147](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/147)
- The same experiment for setup, on the same machines, with a sample photo and three sample recordings; the results are kept in `experiments/speed/`.
- **Larry's timing 8:** processing a photo when it is added: the upload, HEIC conversion if needed, the face checks (R8) and the quality checks and best photo (R9), until the photo shows as checked.
- **Larry's timing 9:** processing each recording when it is added: the upload, conversion to WAV (R2), the sound checks (R10) and the one-speaker check (R11), for each recording, until it shows as checked.
- **Larry's timing 6:** preparing the video and voice: each step of Prepare (R12, with its existing `Prepare step finished` durations), the person's voice made from the recordings (R25), the sample video, Goodbye and Welcome back, and the idle video, until the sample can be watched; and Prepare again after a change of photo, recordings or accent (R28), and for an avatar in the user's own voice (R24d).
- **Added (from the code):** Start now with a default person (R24c): the pack's download and unpacking, the lines rendered, and the warm-up for that person, until the chat opens; and switching to another person, which warms up the replies again before the first prompt is taken.
- **Added (from the code):** the Prepare page and Start now ask for progress every 1 second and 500 ms (`PREPARE_POLL_MS`, `POLL_MS`), so the progress shown can lag by that much.
- **Can show:** a table of where the time goes in setup for the sample photo and recordings and for Start now, with Larry's timings 6, 8 and 9 and the added ones, and the largest costs named; these decide the PRs that speed up setup.
- **Built:** `imageskin time-setup` goes through setup on the running app over its API, as the browser does but without one: a photo and three recordings uploaded, Prepare, Prepare again after another photo is chosen and after another accent (with a voice that has accents), Start now and a switch to another person, each to the chat opening and its first prompt's first word heard, and Prepare for the avatar in your own voice. It runs only on an app with nothing set up yet (or with `--anyway`), since it changes the setup. Each upload's answer now has a `Server-Timing` header with its steps (`save`, `convert`, `wait`, `read`, `faces`, `covered`, `quality`, `sound`, `speakers`, `voice-sample`, `endpoint`; all after FastAPI has received and parsed the upload, so `time-setup` also reports the time before the endpoint), which the browser's developer tools show too. In a cloud session a photo and a recording were each checked in about a second, and Prepare took 6.2 minutes, two thirds of it the video of the person listening (252 s) and a fifth the mouth shapes (69 s), and as long again after another photo; Start now couldn't download a person there, so it, the switch and the avatar in your own voice wait for a run on Larry's hosted machine. On Larry's hosted machine (2026-10-23) every step passed: a photo was checked in 0.6 to 0.7 s and a recording in 1.4 s; Prepare took 51.7 minutes, 38 of them (73%) the video of the person listening and 11 the mouth shapes, both 9 times the cloud's, and as long again after another photo; another accent or the avatar in your own voice took about 2.5 minutes; Start now opened the chat in 22 s and a switch of person in 18 s, with the first word heard 6 to 8 s later. These decide R96e. The results are in `experiments/speed/`.

### R96c. Where the time goes: start-up and the first load (Medium) · Speed · Done in [PR #150](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/150)
- The same experiment for starting the app, on the same machines, from `docker compose up` on the hosted machine and `imageskin serve` in a cloud session; the results are kept in `experiments/speed/`.
- **Larry's timing 10:** from the app starting to the LLM being ready: llama-server loading the model, then the first prompt's warm-up (R22a), until a prompt is answered at the usual speed.
- **Larry's timing 7:** the first load of everything: the server's start until it logs "Loading has completed. The application is ready for use.", broken into its parts (the face models, the speaker model, and the replies' warm-up: the voice, the video and the LLM), and the browser's first load of the app (the page, `app.js`, the fonts and the 60 face pictures) until the chat or setup can be used. The least important of the ten, as it happens once (Larry, 2026-10-21), but each part that could start sooner or load later is named.
- **Added (from the code):** changing the LLM in Settings (R42): from saving the new choice until it answers, with the local LLM's warm-up again.
- **Added (from the code):** coming back after a restart: from opening the app to Welcome back playing, with the saved setup loaded (R30).
- **Can show:** a table of where the time goes from start to the first reply, and in the browser's first load, with Larry's timings 7 and 10 and the added ones, and the parts that could be sped up named; these decide the PRs that speed up start-up.
- **Built:** `imageskin time-startup`, run right after the app starts, waits for the ready line and for llama-server's `/health` to answer 200, then reads when each part of the start began and ended from the app's new `/api/startup` (in ms from the process starting: the app made, the face and speaker models, the reply warm-up with the LLM's and the voice and video's, the server listening and the ready line; the log has `Start-up part finished` for each). It then times the first prompt and the next, the browser's first load (the page, scripts, styles, fonts and face pictures, as a browser fetches them), the chat's requests when it is opened again with Welcome back's video, and saving the local LLM again in Settings to its next answer. The tables go to `speed` in the data folder, and the results to `experiments/speed/`. In a cloud session (2026-10-23) the ready line came 46.6 s after the process started, 44 s of it the voice and video warm-up (Chatterbox and the photoreal engine); the LLM's warm-up ran while llama-server was still loading its model, was refused with 503 and wasn't tried again, so the first prompt's first token came after 3.2 s against 1.4 s for the next; the browser's first load was 74 files and 0.5 MB in 0.3 s, and opening the chat again took 54 ms. On Larry's hosted machine the ready line came at 14.7 s, but the first reply after the restart took 65.6 s to its first token against 4.0 s for the next: the warm-up was refused while llama-server loaded, and it covers only the system prompt, not the saved conversation's 924 tokens of history.

### R97. Faster settings for llama-server (Medium) · Speed · Done in [PR #151](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/151)
- An experiment, on the same machine and PDF, comparing llama-server settings: 2, 3 and 4 threads, batch size, flash attention, the model's quantization (Q4 and Q8), Gemma 3 1B for the wiki against 4B, and the context size. Each is judged on the time per PDF, the time to a reply's first sentence, and the wiki's quality (the same topics found, judged against the 4B wiki).
- The best settings go into `deploy/compose.yaml` and the local setup, with the measurements in `experiments/speed/`.
- **Can show:** the sample PDF processed and a reply's first sentence written in less time than R96a and R96 measured, with a wiki of the same quality.
- **Built:** `imageskin time-llm` times a running llama-server as the app asks it (the warm-up, three replies, and the sample PDF's first wiki part) and adds a row for each setting to one table; `experiments/speed/llama_settings.sh` runs it for 15 settings, locally or on the hosted machine with Docker. In a cloud session llama-server read every prompt again from its start, as Gemma 3's sliding-window cache can't be cut back to a shared start, so the warm-up was never reused (the 1 token R96 found); `--swa-full` fixes that, and with `--threads-batch 4` (reading on all 4 cores, writing still on 2) a reply's first token came after 0.35 s instead of 5.1 s and its first sentence after 2.4 s instead of 7.4 s, and the sample PDF's wiki is estimated at 64 minutes instead of 73, with the same topics. Both are now in `deploy/compose.yaml` and the README. 4 threads would write the wiki in 43 minutes but take the voice's cores (for R99), and Gemma 3 1B in 24 minutes with poorer topics, so neither is used; Q8_0 was slower and the rest made no difference. On Larry's hosted machine (4 Ampere cores) the same held, and Q4_0, which llama.cpp speeds up on ARM, built the wiki in 59 minutes against Q4_K_M's 78 (no faster in the cloud), so `deploy/compose.yaml` runs Q4_0 there, with `--threads 2 --threads-batch 4 --swa-full` and 4 cores for the llama container; the README's laptop line keeps Q4_K_M with the same flags. On the hosted machine a reply's first sentence now comes after about 2 s instead of 4.6 to 8.9 s, and 97 tokens of the warm-up are reused instead of 1. The results are in `experiments/speed/`.
- **Follow-up (2026-10-24, [PR #162](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/162)):** llama-server keeps up to 8 GB of earlier prompts in memory by default, and on the hosted machine it grew past the llama container's 6 GB cap during a wiki build and was killed. `--cache-ram 1024` now caps that at 1 GB in `deploy/compose.yaml` and the README's laptop line.

### R98. Fewer, shorter wiki requests (Medium) · items 25, 26 · Done in [PR #156](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/156)
- From R96a's results, the wiki asks the LLM for less: each part's prompt starts with the same text so llama-server reuses what it has already read, the titles found so far come after the part instead of before it, replies are asked to be shorter where they can be, and outline steps that ask about little are joined. The wiki keeps the same topics and pages.
- **Can show:** the sample PDF's wiki built in less time than after R97, with its time per part and the share of the prompt reused shown in the log, and its topics compared with the earlier wiki.
- **Built:** in a cloud session with R97's llama-server settings, the sample PDF's wiki took 35.4 minutes against 53.8 on main, writing 7,148 tokens against 11,854, with the same topics and as many detail pages. Gemma set its JSON out over many lines, one paragraph number a line, and asking in words for one line didn't change that, so llama-server is held to compact JSON by a grammar (`json_grammar.py`), with a line break allowed only between the objects of a list (held to one line there too, it ended a list of topics after the first). A topic's detail request asks only for its parts, as the summary it wrote was thrown away, and a part's summary is 1 or 2 sentences. A part's prompt starts with the instructions and has the titles found so far after the part, and both outline prompts start with the same text. The log has `Wiki step timed` for each part and outline step, with its time and `reused_pct`, and `Wiki pages built` has `llm_reused_pct`. Small outline steps are not joined: since R97 their instructions are reused, so it would save almost nothing. On Larry's hosted machine (2026-10-24) the sample PDF's wiki took 20.8 minutes, writing 5,039 tokens, with 18 topics and every answer usable; R96a's run there took about 3 hours, before PR #148 and R97. The results are in `experiments/speed/`.

### R99. Replies don't wait behind the wiki (Simple) · items 12, 26 · Done in [PR #154](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/154)
- While a wiki is built, a chat reply no longer waits for the wiki's current request to finish: llama-server keeps a second slot for replies, or the wiki pauses for the reply and goes on after it, whichever R96a and R97 show is faster on the hosted machine.
- From R97's results: 4 writing threads built the sample PDF's wiki in 41 minutes on the hosted machine against 63 with 2, but would take the voice's cores during a reply, so the wiki could use 4 threads only while nobody is chatting and drop back to 2 when a reply starts.
- **Can show:** a question asked while a PDF's wiki is being built gets its first sentence spoken within the usual time, and the wiki goes on to finish.
- **Built:** the wiki pauses for the reply. llama-server already has 4 slots, and it was the reply and the wiki's request running at once on the same threads that made the reply slow (R96a), so a second slot wouldn't help. While a reply uses the local LLM, the wiki's request in progress is cut off, as when its PDF is removed, no new one starts, and the request is asked again once the reply is written (`RepliesFirst` in `replies_first.py`; the log has `Wiki request cut off for a reply` and `Wiki waited for a reply` with `duration_ms`). llama-server keeps what it read of the cut-off request, so asking again costs little. In a cloud session a reply asked 20 s into the sample PDF's first wiki part had its first sentence after 5.2 s instead of 19.6 s (6.2 s with the LLM idle), and the part finished no later. The 4 threads while nobody chats aren't done: llama-server's threads change only with a restart, which would lose its cache. The results are in `experiments/speed/`.

### R100. From the reply to the spoken word sooner (Medium) · item 12 · Done in [PR #146](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/146) (first part) and [PR #155](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/155) (second part)
- From R96's results, cut the largest wait between the LLM's first sentence and the video saying it (for example, starting the voice on a shorter first clause, keeping the voice and video engines warm, or sending the first frames sooner), measured with the timing readout.
- **Can show:** three replies, each with the first word heard sooner after the first sentence is written than R96 measured.
- **Built (first part):** while every clip so far is made and the LLM is still writing a sentence, the sentence is handed over at its next clause, by the first clause's rule, rather than at its full stop: the gaps after the first clip in R96's cloud run were the voice waiting for the rest of a long first sentence. Where the voice can't keep up (Larry's hosted machine, and a 2.8 GHz cloud session), it is never waiting, so nothing changes there; speaking the next sentence while the clip before is rendered was measured again and is slower. The results are in `experiments/speed/`.
- **Built (second part):** a reply made without the LLM, such as the answer to "What can you talk about?", is cut into clips before it is spoken (the decline's and a lesson's sentences mostly stay whole, being short): its first sentence after its first clause and any sentence longer than 12 words at its clauses, only at a comma, semicolon or colon outside brackets, inline code, math and links, so a title is split only where it has a comma of its own. On Larry's hosted machine the list of topics was one clip of 15 s whose first word was heard 11.9 s after Send (R96a); with Kokoro in a cloud session the first word is spoken after 1.1 s instead of 9.5 s, and each next clip before the one before ends. The log has `Made reply cut into clips`.

### R96e. A quicker video of the person listening (Medium) · items 5, 17 · Speed · Done in [PR #153](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/153)
- Added from R96b's results (2026-10-23): on Larry's hosted machine the video of the person listening, the idle video shown between replies (R23), takes 38 of Prepare's 52 minutes, and it is made again for each new photo. It is made frame by frame by the photoreal engine like any other video, though nothing in it speaks.
- Make it in less time, whichever of these is quickest without a visible join, judged by watching the loop: fewer frames played forwards and then backwards, a shorter loop, or fewer frames rendered by the engine and the ones between blended. If it is still long, the sample video is shown first and the listening video is made after it, with the still photo shown between replies until it is ready.
- **Can show:** Prepare on the hosted machine with the listening video's step much shorter than R96b's 38 minutes, measured with `imageskin time-setup`; the loop beside the old one, with no visible join; and the log's `Prepare step finished` for the step with its duration.
- **Built:** the head's drift in the loop is now made of cosines, so its second half goes back the way the first half came: only frames 0 to 100 are rendered, every 8th one and the middle (14 frames instead of 50), the ones between are filled in along the optical flow, and the second half is the first played backwards. Against fully rendered frames the filled-in ones score 39 to 45 dB PSNR (every 12th would save 4 more frames but drop to 38.6 dB). In a cloud session the loop step took 74 s instead of 256 s, and a whole photo 168 s instead of 349 s; on Larry's hosted machine it took 666 s (11 minutes) instead of R96b's 38 minutes, and a whole photo 23 minutes instead of 49. A loop started by an older version is begun again rather than mixed, a finished one is kept, and default people's packs are unchanged. The log's `Filled in idle loop frames` has the frames rendered, filled in and mirrored, and the loop's time (`loop_s`). The results are in `experiments/idle_loop/`.

### R98a. Each topic's first lesson ready ahead (Medium) · items 12, 54 · Speed · Done in [PR #160](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/160)
- Added by Larry on 2026-10-24, after asking about PR #156: picking a topic sends "Teach me about …", and the LLM writes that lesson from scratch each time (`teach.py`), so "thinking…" runs long, about as long as any answer from the wiki on the hosted machine.
- Once a PDF's wiki is built, the LLM writes the first part of each topic's lesson (its main ideas, as Teach me's first step asks for them) in the background, and it is saved with the wiki. It is written again for a topic whose pages are edited, rebuilt or gone back to, and dropped when the PDF is removed. It never holds up a reply: a prompt waits for no saved lesson, and while one is being written a reply comes first (R99).
- When a participant picks a topic whose first part is saved, the person starts saying it at once, sentence by sentence, as a streamed reply would be; the next parts are written as now. A topic with nothing saved yet is taught as now.
- **Can show:** on the hosted machine, the first word of a picked topic heard within a few seconds instead of R96a's time for "Teach me about", measured with `imageskin time-pdf`; the log's `Lesson saved` with the topic and its time, and `Lesson from the saved part` when one is used.
- **Built:** `lessons_ahead.py` writes each topic's first part in the background, with the chat's system prompt and none of the conversation, and saves it in the wiki's folder (`lessons.json`) with a key made from what the LLM was asked: the instruction and its material. A picked topic (or Next into one, or Teach me everything) is said from the saved part only while the lesson would ask exactly that, so an edited, rebuilt, restored or loaded topic is taught by the LLM as before until its part is written again, and a removed PDF's topics are dropped at the next round. A round runs after the wiki queue is built, after any change to the wiki, at the start and when Teach me is turned on; it does nothing while Teach me is off, waits while a wiki is being built, stops for a build that starts partway, and tries again after 60 s if the LLM can't be reached. Its requests go after the chat's (R99), so a reply cuts one off and it is asked again after. `GET /api/lessons` says whether it is writing, whether every topic's part is saved (`ready`) and the topics saved, and `imageskin time-pdf` waits for `ready` before timing Teach me. With a slow fake LLM (3 s an answer), "Teach me about" took 3.0 s without the saved part and 0.01 s with it. On Larry's hosted machine the sample PDF's 30 topics were written in 8 minutes after its wiki (about 17 s each), and a picked topic's first word was heard after 2.8 and 5.3 s, against 14.3 to 15.8 s for answers the LLM writes; the rest is the first clip's voice (`experiments/speed/lessons-r98a-host-2026-10-24.md`). `time-pdf` now asks Teach me about the outline's first topic (the first page's title wasn't one) and waits for the conversation summary made after its questions (`GET /api/chat/summary`), which held it up 87 s in that run. The log has `Lesson saved` (`topic`, `duration_ms`), `Lessons written ahead`, `Lessons dropped`, `Lesson not saved` and `Lesson from the saved part`.

### R98b. Every paragraph of the PDF in the wiki (Simple) · items 25, 26 · Content · Done in [PR #161](https://github.com/larry94555/ImageSkinForLLM/pull/161)
- Added by Larry on 2026-10-24: he is fine with the wiki's build time as long as no information is lost. The wiki's lowest level is the PDF's own paragraphs, but when the LLM groups a topic's paragraphs into parts it may leave one out as not about the topic (`DETAIL_PROMPT`), and a paragraph on a page no topic names is in no topic at all.
- After the outline is built, each paragraph of the PDF that is in no entry is put back: in the entry for its page's topic, at its place in the PDF's order, or, if no topic has its page, in an entry of its own titled from its page. No LLM request is needed.
- **Can show:** the sample PDF's wiki with every paragraph of the PDF in it, checked by a unit test that counts them; the log's `Wiki paragraphs put back` with how many and their pages.
- **Built:** `Outliner.put_back` in `wiki_outline.py`, run at the end of every outline. Paragraphs next to each other on the same page go back as one entry, "More from page N" in the first topic that has the page, or "Page N" where no topic has the page, placed by where they start in the PDF: on the first level, where it is one of the topics, or, when it falls between the topics of one section, inside that section among them, where it is not. Two paragraphs with the same words are kept apart by their place in the PDF. A topic the LLM couldn't split gets its paragraphs back the same way; a topic whose pages say little still shows its own few sentences, as R37a made it. Built again from R98's recorded answers, the sample PDF's wiki had 37 of its 366 paragraphs in no entry, all of page 13 ("Open questions about primes") among them; now all 366 are in it, in 8 entries put back (`test_the_sample_pdfs_wiki_keeps_every_paragraph_of_the_pdf`). The log has `Wiki paragraphs put back` with `paragraphs` and `pages`.
